Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, Dr. Smith. Well, my husband wanted me to come in, but really it’s about the kitchen. For nearly two years now, I’ve been absolutely sure that our contractor and some of the people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us. They're keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. 

Which is just... well, two years is a long time. That’s right around when my sister got her new Shih Tzu, little Barnaby. Oh, he is a terror. She paid an absolute fortune for him from some breeder in Ohio, and he just chews through everything. He chewed right through her good sensible walking shoes, the ones with the arch support she got at Macy's. I told her she should have rescued a senior dog. I've always preferred older dogs, they just sit with you and watch television, you know? The young ones just have too much nervous energy. 

But anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right, the kitchen. No matter how many receipts my husband shows me or how many excuses those people give about supply chains or whatever, I know they're in on it together.

Hearing things? Oh heavens no, my mind is perfectly sound. No, no, I don't see or hear anything that isn't there. My hearing is sharp as a tack, actually. If anything, it's my husband who really needs to get his ears checked. 

I keep telling him he needs to go down to Costco and get fitted for those little hearing aids. No, heavens no. I've never had a seizure or a head injury or anything wrong with my brain. My brain is perfectly healthy. I actually have a wonderful memory, much better than my sister's, that's for sure. I remember every single birthday in the family without having to write them down. 

My nephew David, he just turned 40 last Tuesday. 40! I can still picture him running around in his little footie pajamas playing with those Matchbox cars. He had this big orange plastic track that went all the way through the living room, and if you weren't looking where you were walking, you'd step right on one of those little metal cars in your bare feet. Oh, it would hurt so bad! Worse than stepping on a Lego, I tell you. 

That's exactly why I always wear my slippers around the house now. I have these lovely memory foam ones I found at Kohl's. They were on clearance, plus I had my 30 percent off coupon they send in the mail. I still like getting the physical mail, though lately it's just been endless flyers for replacement windows and those terrible political postcards. They just fill up the recycling bin.

I really only use it to play Solitaire now. The classic one with the green background, you know, not that Vegas scoring one. I never understood the point of the Vegas scoring, it just makes you feel like you're losing money. \n\nBut... oh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? I completely lost my train of thought.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with a neurological condition, such as a head injury, seizure disorder, or any other medical illness that affects your brain?\n\nPatient: No, heavens no. I've never had a seizure or a head injury or anything wrong with my brain. My brain is perfectly healthy. I actually have a wonderful memory, much better than my sister's, that's for sure. I remember every single birthday in the family without having to write them down. \n\nMy nephew David, he just turned 40 last Tuesday. 40! I can still picture him running around in his little footie pajamas playing with those Matchbox cars. He had this big orange plastic track that went all the way through the living room, and if you weren't looking where you were walking, you'd step right on one of those little metal cars in your bare feet. Oh, it would hurt so bad! Worse than stepping on a Lego, I tell you. \n\nThat's exactly why I always wear my slippers around the house now. I have these lovely memory foam ones I found at Kohl's. They were on clearance, plus I had my 30 percent off coupon they send in the mail. I still like getting the physical mail, though lately it's just been endless flyers for replacement windows and those terrible political postcards. They just fill up the recycling bin. \n\nBut... wait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about David? No, you were asking about... oh, my health. Right. No, no brain conditions at all.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what the date is today, including the year?\n\nPatient: Oh, the date. Yes, it's Tuesday... or is it Wednesday? Let me think. It's November 14th, 2023. I know it's November because I just bought a beautiful new wreath for the front door. The old one was looking so ragged, the artificial berries were just flaking off everywhere. I found this new one at HomeGoods, and it has these lovely frosted pinecones and little faux cardinal birds tucked into the branches. \n\nCardinals have always been my favorite. My grandmother used to tell me that when you see a cardinal, it means a visitor from heaven is checking in on you. I don't know if I believe all that, but they are just so striking against the snow. We haven't had much snow yet this year, though. Just that one dusting last week that barely stuck to the grass. The weatherman on Channel 4—the new one with the very white teeth, I can't stand him, he talks too fast—he said it's going to be a mild winter. I hope so, because shoveling the driveway just aggravates my lower back. I have that heating pad I bought at the pharmacy, but the cord is so short I have to sit right next to the outlet in the living room... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? You asked the date. November 14th, 2023.\n\n[Last Question]\nCould you please count backward from 20 by subtracting 3 each time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Subtracting 3... alright, let me think. So, 20... then 17... and then 14... 

14. You know, Channel 14 used to have all those wonderful old game shows. I used to watch Jeopardy every single night. We'd eat our dinner right on those little wooden TV trays in the living room. Do they even make TV trays anymore? The ones we had were real wood, heavy, not like that flimsy plastic junk they sell at Target now. 

I bought a plastic storage bin at Target just last week to organize my knitting yarn, and the handle snapped right off before I even got it out to the parking lot. I had to march right back inside to the customer service desk. The girl working there—she couldn't have been more than sixteen, chewing gum like a cow with cud—she looked at me like I was the one who broke it on purpose! I told her, 'Honey, I just bought this two minutes ago.' She had to call a manager over, and he had to use this special little key on the register... it took twenty minutes just to get my eight dollars back. I could have knitted half a scarf in the time I stood there waiting.

No, no brain conditions at all.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what the date is today, including the year?\n\nPatient: Oh, the date. Yes, it's Tuesday... or is it Wednesday? Let me think. It's November 14th, 2023. I know it's November because I just bought a beautiful new wreath for the front door. The old one was looking so ragged, the artificial berries were just flaking off everywhere. I found this new one at HomeGoods, and it has these lovely frosted pinecones and little faux cardinal birds tucked into the branches. \n\nCardinals have always been my favorite. My grandmother used to tell me that when you see a cardinal, it means a visitor from heaven is checking in on you. I don't know if I believe all that, but they are just so striking against the snow. We haven't had much snow yet this year, though. Just that one dusting last week that barely stuck to the grass. The weatherman on Channel 4—the new one with the very white teeth, I can't stand him, he talks too fast—he said it's going to be a mild winter. I hope so, because shoveling the driveway just aggravates my lower back. I have that heating pad I bought at the pharmacy, but the cord is so short I have to sit right next to the outlet in the living room... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? So, 20... then 17... and then 14... \n\n14. You know, Channel 14 used to have all those wonderful old game shows. I used to watch Jeopardy every single night. We'd eat our dinner right on those little wooden TV trays in the living room. Do they even make TV trays anymore? The ones we had were real wood, heavy, not like that flimsy plastic junk they sell at Target now. \n\nI bought a plastic storage bin at Target just last week to organize my knitting yarn, and the handle snapped right off before I even got it out to the parking lot. I had to march right back inside to the customer service desk. The girl working there—she couldn't have been more than sixteen, chewing gum like a cow with cud—she looked at me like I was the one who broke it on purpose! I told her, 'Honey, I just bought this two minutes ago.' She had to call a manager over, and he had to use this special little key on the register... it took twenty minutes just to get my eight dollars back. I could have knitted half a scarf in the time I stood there waiting. \n\nAnyway, I finally got my money back and just went to JoAnn Fabrics instead. They always have better quality things, and they don't give you any grief about returns... wait, what were we doing? Math? Oh dear, I'm sorry. You wanted me to count, didn't you? Where was I?\n\n[Last Question]\nCould you please tell me what you had for breakfast this morning?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Breakfast? Oh, I think I just had a cup of coffee this morning. I have one of those Keurig machines now. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. I told her I didn't need anything so fancy, I was perfectly fine with my old Mr. Coffee drip maker, but she insisted. It uses those little plastic pods, which are terribly convenient, but I always feel so guilty throwing them in the trash. 

You hear so much about the plastic in the ocean these days, and those poor sea turtles. I watched a whole documentary about it on PBS last month. It was narrated by that nice British man, David Attenborough. He has such a soothing voice, doesn't he? I could listen to him read the phone book. 

Though I suppose they don't really print phone books anymore, do they? The last one they dropped at the end of my driveway was so thin, it was practically a pamphlet. And it got completely soaked because the delivery boy didn't even bother to put it in a plastic bag, he just threw it right in a puddle. Kids today just don't take any pride in their work. When I got my first job at the bakery downtown right out of high school, I made sure every single pastry box was tied perfectly with baker's twine... 

Oh, excuse me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about the bakery?

I could have knitted half a scarf in the time I stood there waiting. \n\nAnyway, I finally got my money back and just went to JoAnn Fabrics instead. Where was I?\n\nDoctor: Could you please tell me what you had for breakfast this morning?\n\nPatient: Breakfast? Oh, I think I just had a cup of coffee this morning. I have one of those Keurig machines now. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. I told her I didn't need anything so fancy, I was perfectly fine with my old Mr. Coffee drip maker, but she insisted. It uses those little plastic pods, which are terribly convenient, but I always feel so guilty throwing them in the trash. \n\nYou hear so much about the plastic in the ocean these days, and those poor sea turtles. I watched a whole documentary about it on PBS last month. It was narrated by that nice British man, David Attenborough. He has such a soothing voice, doesn't he? I could listen to him read the phone book. \n\nThough I suppose they don't really print phone books anymore, do they? The last one they dropped at the end of my driveway was so thin, it was practically a pamphlet. And it got completely soaked because the delivery boy didn't even bother to put it in a plastic bag, he just threw it right in a puddle. Kids today just don't take any pride in their work. When I got my first job at the bakery downtown right out of high school, I made sure every single pastry box was tied perfectly with baker's twine... \n\nOh, excuse me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about the bakery?\n\n[Last Question]\nEarlier, I mentioned a few things to you, but I'd like to try something different: could you please remember these three words for me—Apple, Table, and Penny—and I will ask you to repeat them back to me in a few minutes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Apple, table, penny. Yes, I can remember those. Apple... you know, I used to make the most wonderful apple pies from scratch. My husband, God rest his soul, he would always ask for one on his birthday instead of a cake. He said cakes were too dry, but a good pie, especially with a crumble topping—none of that double-crust nonsense, it has to be a crumble with brown sugar and plenty of butter—well, he just loved it. 

I always used Granny Smith apples. The red ones turn to complete mush when you bake them. I learned that the hard way when I first got married. I made a pie for my mother-in-law using Red Delicious, and it came out looking like applesauce in a pastry shell. She didn't say a word, of course, she just took one bite, gave me that tight little smile she always did, and pushed her plate away. She was a very particular woman. Her house was always spotless, you could eat off the floors. She used to iron her sheets. Can you imagine? Who has the time to iron bedsheets? 

I certainly don't. Although I suppose I have plenty of time now, since I'm not working anymore. I retired from the bakery... well, it wasn't the bakery I mentioned before, it was the front office at the dental clinic. I worked there for twenty-two years. Dr. Miller was a saint, but his partner, Dr. Evans... oh, he was a piece of work. Always snapping at the hygienists... 

Wait, what were the words again? Apple, table... and... oh, I've lost the last one. Penny! That was it. Apple, table, penny. I'll try to hold onto them.

It uses those little plastic pods, which are terribly convenient, but I always feel so guilty throwing them in the trash. \n\nYou hear so much about the plastic in the ocean these days, and those poor sea turtles. I watched a whole documentary about it on PBS last month. It was narrated by that nice British man, David Attenborough. He has such a soothing voice, doesn't he? I could listen to him read the phone book. \n\nThough I suppose they don't really print phone books anymore, do they? The last one they dropped at the end of my driveway was so thin, it was practically a pamphlet. And it got completely soaked because the delivery boy didn't even bother to put it in a plastic bag, he just threw it right in a puddle. Kids today just don't take any pride in their work. When I got my first job at the bakery downtown right out of high school, I made sure every single pastry box was tied perfectly with baker's twine... \n\nOh, excuse me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? Yes, I can remember those. Apple... you know, I used to make the most wonderful apple pies from scratch. My husband, God rest his soul, he would always ask for one on his birthday instead of a cake. He said cakes were too dry, but a good pie, especially with a crumble topping—none of that double-crust nonsense, it has to be a crumble with brown sugar and plenty of butter—well, he just loved it. \n\nI always used Granny Smith apples. The red ones turn to complete mush when you bake them. I learned that the hard way when I first got married. I made a pie for my mother-in-law using Red Delicious, and it came out looking like applesauce in a pastry shell. She didn't say a word, of course, she just took one bite, gave me that tight little smile she always did, and pushed her plate away. She was a very particular woman. Her house was always spotless, you could eat off the floors. She used to iron her sheets. Can you imagine? Who has the time to iron bedsheets? \n\nI certainly don't. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I just sit down at the kitchen table on the first of the month with my checkbook. I still write paper checks for the water and the electric. My daughter keeps telling me to do it all online, but I just don't trust putting my bank numbers on the internet. You hear so many stories about hackers these days. Just last week on the evening news, they were talking about people having their whole identities stolen by teenagers sitting in basements halfway across the world. It's terrifying, really. 

That's why I like my paper checks. Though I will say, the mail delivery has been terribly inconsistent lately. Our regular mailman, Gary, retired in April. He was wonderful, always made sure the envelopes weren't bent or left out in the rain. He told me he was moving to Florida to live near his sister. Florida is much too humid for me, though. My hair just frizzes right up the second I step off the plane in Orlando. We went to Disney World back in 1988, and it rained every single afternoon at exactly three o'clock... 

But anyway, my day-to-day life is pretty steady. Outside of dealing with the remodel, I still keep up with my church gatherings, book club, household routines, and family plans the way I usually do. We're reading a historical fiction novel for book club this month. It's about World War II, which is fine, but Mary—she's the one who picked it—always chooses these terribly depressing stories. I prefer a nice mystery, like Agatha Christie. They just don't write mysteries like they used to, do they? It's all so violent and graphic on television now... 

Oh dear, I'm sorry, doctor. I'm rambling about Mary's books. Did you ask me about my bills? Yes, I just write the checks and put them in the mailbox.

Apple, table... and... oh, I've lost the last one. Penny! That was it. Apple, table, penny. I'll try to hold onto them.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me how you go about managing your monthly household bills and finances?\n\nPatient: Well, I just sit down at the kitchen table on the first of the month with my checkbook. I still write paper checks for the water and the electric. My daughter keeps telling me to do it all online, but I just don't trust putting my bank numbers on the internet. You hear so many stories about hackers these days. Just last week on the evening news, they were talking about people having their whole identities stolen by teenagers sitting in basements halfway across the world. It's terrifying, really. \n\nThat's why I like my paper checks. Though I will say, the mail delivery has been terribly inconsistent lately. Our regular mailman, Gary, retired in April. He was wonderful, always made sure the envelopes weren't bent or left out in the rain. He told me he was moving to Florida to live near his sister. Florida is much too humid for me, though. My hair just frizzes right up the second I step off the plane in Orlando. We went to Disney World back in 1988, and it rained every single afternoon at exactly three o'clock... \n\nBut anyway, my day-to-day life is pretty steady. Outside of dealing with the remodel, I still keep up with my church gatherings, book club, household routines, and family plans the way I usually do. We're reading a historical fiction novel for book club this month. It's about World War II, which is fine, but Mary—she's the one who picked it—always chooses these terribly depressing stories. I prefer a nice mystery, like Agatha Christie. They just don't write mysteries like they used to, do they? It's all so violent and graphic on television now... \n\nOh dear, I'm sorry, doctor. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Three words... oh, yes, you did ask me to remember those, didn't you? Let me just think for a second. I know I repeated them back to you... it's funny how things just slip right out of your mind these days. 

My daughter keeps telling me I need to do those Sudoku puzzles to keep my brain sharp. She bought me a whole thick book of them for Christmas, but I just can't make heads or tails of all those numbers. I've always preferred crosswords. The ones in the local paper are just about my speed, though they recently changed the editor and the clues have gotten so terribly strange. Last Tuesday, there was a clue about some popular rap music singer, and how on earth am I supposed to know that? I mostly just listen to the oldies station. They play Frank Sinatra on Sunday mornings. My husband and I used to dance in the kitchen to Frank Sinatra while the Sunday roast was in the oven. He was such a wonderful dancer... he had two left feet when we first met, but he actually took secret lessons just for our wedding. The look on my mother's face when he spun me around the dance floor... 

Oh, goodness. I'm sorry, doctor. The words. I'm afraid they've completely vanished. I don't remember a single one of them.

He chewed right through her good sensible walking shoes, the ones with the arch support she got at Macy's. I told her she should have rescued a senior dog. I remember every single birthday in the family without having to write them down. \n\nMy nephew David, he just turned 40 last Tuesday. 40! I can still picture him running around in his little footie pajamas playing with those Matchbox cars. No, no brain conditions at all.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what the date is today, including the year?\n\nPatient: Oh, the date. Yes, it's Tuesday... or is it Wednesday? Let me think. It's November 14th, 2023. I know it's November because I just bought a beautiful new wreath for the front door. The old one was looking so ragged, the artificial berries were just flaking off everywhere. I found this new one at HomeGoods, and it has these lovely frosted pinecones and little faux cardinal birds tucked into the branches. \n\nCardinals have always been my favorite. My grandmother used to tell me that when you see a cardinal, it means a visitor from heaven is checking in on you. I don't know if I believe all that, but they are just so striking against the snow. We haven't had much snow yet this year, though. Just that one dusting last week that barely stuck to the grass. The weatherman on Channel 4—the new one with the very white teeth, I can't stand him, he talks too fast—he said it's going to be a mild winter. I hope so, because shoveling the driveway just aggravates my lower back. I have that heating pad I bought at the pharmacy, but the cord is so short I have to sit right next to the outlet in the living room... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? You asked the date. November 14th, 2023.\n\nDoctor: Could you please count backward from 20 by subtracting 3 each time?\n\nPatient: Subtracting 3... alright, let me think. So, 20... then 17... and then 14... \n\n14. You know, Channel 14 used to have all those wonderful old game shows. I used to watch Jeopardy every single night. We'd eat our dinner right on those little wooden TV trays in the living room. Do they even make TV trays anymore? The ones we had were real wood, heavy, not like that flimsy plastic junk they sell at Target now. \n\nI bought a plastic storage bin at Target just last week to organize my knitting yarn, and the handle snapped right off before I even got it out to the parking lot. I had to march right back inside to the customer service desk. The girl working there—she couldn't have been more than sixteen, chewing gum like a cow with cud—she looked at me like I was the one who broke it on purpose! I told her, 'Honey, I just bought this two minutes ago.' She had to call a manager over, and he had to use this special little key on the register... it took twenty minutes just to get my eight dollars back. I could have knitted half a scarf in the time I stood there waiting. \n\nAnyway, I finally got my money back and just went to JoAnn Fabrics instead. They always have better quality things, and they don't give you any grief about returns... wait, what were we doing? Math? Oh dear, I'm sorry. You wanted me to count, didn't you? Where was I?\n\nDoctor: Could you please tell me what you had for breakfast this morning?\n\nPatient: Breakfast? Oh, I think I just had a cup of coffee this morning. I have one of those Keurig machines now. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. I told her I didn't need anything so fancy, I was perfectly fine with my old Mr. Coffee drip maker, but she insisted. It uses those little plastic pods, which are terribly convenient, but I always feel so guilty throwing them in the trash. \n\nYou hear so much about the plastic in the ocean these days, and those poor sea turtles. I watched a whole documentary about it on PBS last month. It was narrated by that nice British man, David Attenborough. He has such a soothing voice, doesn't he? I could listen to him read the phone book. \n\nThough I suppose they don't really print phone books anymore, do they? The last one they dropped at the end of my driveway was so thin, it was practically a pamphlet. And it got completely soaked because the delivery boy didn't even bother to put it in a plastic bag, he just threw it right in a puddle. Kids today just don't take any pride in their work. When I got my first job at the bakery downtown right out of high school, I made sure every single pastry box was tied perfectly with baker's twine... \n\nOh, excuse me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about the bakery?\n\nDoctor: Earlier, I mentioned a few things to you, but I'd like to try something different: could you please remember these three words for me—Apple, Table, and Penny—and I will ask you to repeat them back to me in a few minutes?\n\nPatient: Apple, table, penny. Yes, I can remember those. Apple... you know, I used to make the most wonderful apple pies from scratch. My husband, God rest his soul, he would always ask for one on his birthday instead of a cake. He said cakes were too dry, but a good pie, especially with a crumble topping—none of that double-crust nonsense, it has to be a crumble with brown sugar and plenty of butter—well, he just loved it. \n\nI always used Granny Smith apples. The red ones turn to complete mush when you bake them. I learned that the hard way when I first got married. I made a pie for my mother-in-law using Red Delicious, and it came out looking like applesauce in a pastry shell. She didn't say a word, of course, she just took one bite, gave me that tight little smile she always did, and pushed her plate away. She was a very particular woman. Her house was always spotless, you could eat off the floors. She used to iron her sheets. Can you imagine? Who has the time to iron bedsheets? \n\nI certainly don't. Although I suppose I have plenty of time now, since I'm not working anymore. I retired from the bakery... well, it wasn't the bakery I mentioned before, it was the front office at the dental clinic. I worked there for twenty-two years. Dr. Miller was a saint, but his partner, Dr. Evans... oh, he was a piece of work. Always snapping at the hygienists... \n\nWait, what were the words again? Apple, table... and... oh, I've lost the last one. Penny! That was it. Apple, table, penny. I'll try to hold onto them.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me how you go about managing your monthly household bills and finances?\n\nPatient: Well, I just sit down at the kitchen table on the first of the month with my checkbook. He was wonderful, always made sure the envelopes weren't bent or left out in the rain. He told me he was moving to Florida to live near his sister. Florida is much too humid for me, though. My hair just frizzes right up the second I step off the plane in Orlando. We went to Disney World back in 1988, and it rained every single afternoon at exactly three o'clock... \n\nBut anyway, my day-to-day life is pretty steady. Well, yes, I do all of that myself. I cook, I clean, I do the laundry. I've always been very particular about my laundry, especially the whites. You have to use just a splash of bleach, but not too much or it ruins the fibers. My neighbor, Susan, she uses those little plastic pod things you just throw in the machine. I don't trust them. How do you know what's really in them? Plus, I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office—not Dr. Miller's office, my new dentist, Dr. Patel, he's very gentle—anyway, the article said those pods are terrible for the environment. 

Speaking of the environment, have you noticed how strange the weather has been lately? It was so warm last week, I actually went out and pruned my rosebushes. I have a beautiful climbing rose on the trellis by the back door. It's called a 'New Dawn' rose, a lovely pale pink. My grandmother had them in her garden when I was a little girl. She lived in this big Victorian house with a wraparound porch, and we would sit out there in the evenings drinking lemonade. Real lemonade, not that powdered stuff. 

Oh, but you were asking about chores. Yes, I manage just fine. I still make a full dinner every night, even if it's just for me. Last night I made a nice meatloaf.

I had to march right back inside to the customer service desk. The girl working there—she couldn't have been more than sixteen, chewing gum like a cow with cud—she looked at me like I was the one who broke it on purpose! I told her, 'Honey, I just bought this two minutes ago.' We went to Disney World back in 1988, and it rained every single afternoon at exactly three o'clock... \n\nBut anyway, my day-to-day life is pretty steady. Outside of dealing with the remodel, I still keep up with my church gatherings, book club, household routines, and family plans the way I usually do. We're reading a historical fiction novel for book club this month. It's about World War II, which is fine, but Mary—she's the one who picked it—always chooses these terribly depressing stories. I prefer a nice mystery, like Agatha Christie. They just don't write mysteries like they used to, do they? It's all so violent and graphic on television now... \n\nOh dear, I'm sorry, doctor. I'm rambling about Mary's books. Did you ask me about my bills? Yes, I just write the checks and put them in the mailbox.\n\nDoctor: Could you please repeat those three words I asked you to remember a moment ago?\n\nPatient: Three words... oh, yes, you did ask me to remember those, didn't you? She bought me a whole thick book of them for Christmas, but I just can't make heads or tails of all those numbers. I've always preferred crosswords. The ones in the local paper are just about my speed, though they recently changed the editor and the clues have gotten so terribly strange. Last Tuesday, there was a clue about some popular rap music singer, and how on earth am I supposed to know that? I mostly just listen to the oldies station. They play Frank Sinatra on Sunday mornings. My husband and I used to dance in the kitchen to Frank Sinatra while the Sunday roast was in the oven. He was such a wonderful dancer... he had two left feet when we first met, but he actually took secret lessons just for our wedding. The look on my mother's face when he spun me around the dance floor... \n\nOh, goodness. I'm sorry, doctor. The words. I'm afraid they've completely vanished. I don't remember a single one of them.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to manage all of your own household chores, such as cooking full meals, cleaning the house, and doing your own laundry without any help?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do all of that myself. I cook, I clean, I do the laundry. I've always been very particular about my laundry, especially the whites. You have to use just a splash of bleach, but not too much or it ruins the fibers. My neighbor, Susan, she uses those little plastic pod things you just throw in the machine. I don't trust them. How do you know what's really in them? Plus, I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office—not Dr. Miller's office, my new dentist, Dr. Patel, he's very gentle—anyway, the article said those pods are terrible for the environment. \n\nSpeaking of the environment, have you noticed how strange the weather has been lately? Last Tuesday, there was a clue about some popular rap music singer, and how on earth am I supposed to know that? I mostly just listen to the oldies station. They play Frank Sinatra on Sunday mornings. My husband and I used to dance in the kitchen to Frank Sinatra while the Sunday roast was in the oven. He was such a wonderful dancer... he had two left feet when we first met, but he actually took secret lessons just for our wedding. The look on my mother's face when he spun me around the dance floor... \n\nOh, goodness. I'm sorry, doctor. The words. I'm afraid they've completely vanished. I don't remember a single one of them.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to manage all of your own household chores, such as cooking full meals, cleaning the house, and doing your own laundry without any help?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do all of that myself. I cook, I clean, I do the laundry. I've always been very particular about my laundry, especially the whites. You have to use just a splash of bleach, but not too much or it ruins the fibers. My neighbor, Susan, she uses those little plastic pod things you just throw in the machine. I don't trust them. How do you know what's really in them? Plus, I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office—not Dr. Miller's office, my new dentist, Dr. Patel, he's very gentle—anyway, the article said those pods are terrible for the environment. \n\nSpeaking of the environment, have you noticed how strange the weather has been lately? It was so warm last week, I actually went out and pruned my rosebushes. I have a beautiful climbing rose on the trellis by the back door. It's called a 'New Dawn' rose, a lovely pale pink. My grandmother had them in her garden when I was a little girl. She lived in this big Victorian house with a wraparound porch, and we would sit out there in the evenings drinking lemonade. Real lemonade, not that powdered stuff. \n\nOh, but you were asking about chores. Yes, I manage just fine. I still make a full dinner every night, even if it's just for me. Last night I made a nice meatloaf.\n\n[Last Question]\nI am a little confused—earlier you mentioned your husband showing you receipts, but you also mentioned he has passed away; could you clarify if your husband is currently living with you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm not really sure what you're asking me, doctor, or why that's confusing to you. We were just talking about the receipts. 

But goodness, speaking of receipts, have you noticed how ridiculously long they make them at the pharmacy these days? I went in on Tuesday just to buy some dental floss—the waxed kind, mind you, because the unwaxed always frays and gets stuck between my back molars. Anyway, I bought this one little plastic container of floss, and the young girl at the register handed me a piece of paper that must have been three feet long! All those coupons printed on the back that nobody ever looks at. It's just a terrible waste of trees. 

My husband always hated waste. He grew up during the tail end of the Depression, you see, so they saved absolutely everything in his house. Tin foil, rubber bands, those little square plastic tabs that come on the bread loaves... I actually still have a whole drawer in the kitchen just full of bread tabs. Sometimes I use them to scrape baked-on cheese off the casserole dishes. They work like a charm without scratching the glass. I make a wonderful tuna noodle casserole, by the way. I use the crushed-up potato chips on top to give it a nice salty crust. My daughter says nobody eats casseroles anymore and that they're out of style, but I think that's just silly. It's comfort food, plain and simple. Do you like casseroles, doctor?
=====================================

It's nice to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Dr. Smith. Well, my husband wanted me to come in, but really it’s about the kitchen. They're keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. \n\nWhich is just... well, two years is a long time. That’s right around when my sister got her new Shih Tzu, little Barnaby. Oh, he is a terror. He chewed right through her good sensible walking shoes, the ones with the arch support she got at Macy's. I told her she should have rescued a senior dog. No matter how many receipts my husband shows me or how many excuses those people give about supply chains or whatever, I know they're in on it together.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your concerns about the contractor, have you been experiencing any unusual sensations, or hearing or seeing things that others around you do not seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Hearing things? Oh heavens no, my mind is perfectly sound. No, no, I don't see or hear anything that isn't there. My hearing is sharp as a tack, actually. No, no brain conditions at all.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what the date is today, including the year?\n\nPatient: Oh, the date. Yes, it's Tuesday... or is it Wednesday? Let me think. It's November 14th, 2023. I know it's November because I just bought a beautiful new wreath for the front door. The old one was looking so ragged, the artificial berries were just flaking off everywhere. I found this new one at HomeGoods, and it has these lovely frosted pinecones and little faux cardinal birds tucked into the branches. \n\nCardinals have always been my favorite. My grandmother used to tell me that when you see a cardinal, it means a visitor from heaven is checking in on you. I don't know if I believe all that, but they are just so striking against the snow. The weatherman on Channel 4—the new one with the very white teeth, I can't stand him, he talks too fast—he said it's going to be a mild winter. I have that heating pad I bought at the pharmacy, but the cord is so short I have to sit right next to the outlet in the living room... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? You asked the date. November 14th, 2023.\n\nDoctor: Could you please count backward from 20 by subtracting 3 each time?\n\nPatient: Subtracting 3... alright, let me think. So, 20... then 17... and then 14... \n\n14. You know, Channel 14 used to have all those wonderful old game shows. I used to watch Jeopardy every single night. We'd eat our dinner right on those little wooden TV trays in the living room. Do they even make TV trays anymore? The ones we had were real wood, heavy, not like that flimsy plastic junk they sell at Target now. \n\nI bought a plastic storage bin at Target just last week to organize my knitting yarn, and the handle snapped right off before I even got it out to the parking lot. I had to march right back inside to the customer service desk. The girl working there—she couldn't have been more than sixteen, chewing gum like a cow with cud—she looked at me like I was the one who broke it on purpose! I told her, 'Honey, I just bought this two minutes ago.' She had to call a manager over, and he had to use this special little key on the register... it took twenty minutes just to get my eight dollars back. I could have knitted half a scarf in the time I stood there waiting. \n\nAnyway, I finally got my money back and just went to JoAnn Fabrics instead. They always have better quality things, and they don't give you any grief about returns... wait, what were we doing? Math? Oh dear, I'm sorry. You wanted me to count, didn't you? Where was I?\n\nDoctor: Could you please tell me what you had for breakfast this morning?\n\nPatient: Breakfast? Oh, I think I just had a cup of coffee this morning. I have one of those Keurig machines now. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. I told her I didn't need anything so fancy, I was perfectly fine with my old Mr. Coffee drip maker, but she insisted. It uses those little plastic pods, which are terribly convenient, but I always feel so guilty throwing them in the trash. \n\nYou hear so much about the plastic in the ocean these days, and those poor sea turtles. I watched a whole documentary about it on PBS last month. It was narrated by that nice British man, David Attenborough. He has such a soothing voice, doesn't he? I could listen to him read the phone book. \n\nThough I suppose they don't really print phone books anymore, do they? The last one they dropped at the end of my driveway was so thin, it was practically a pamphlet. When I got my first job at the bakery downtown right out of high school, I made sure every single pastry box was tied perfectly with baker's twine... \n\nOh, excuse me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about the bakery?\n\nDoctor: Earlier, I mentioned a few things to you, but I'd like to try something different: could you please remember these three words for me—Apple, Table, and Penny—and I will ask you to repeat them back to me in a few minutes?\n\nPatient: Apple, table, penny. Yes, I can remember those. Apple... you know, I used to make the most wonderful apple pies from scratch. My husband, God rest his soul, he would always ask for one on his birthday instead of a cake. He said cakes were too dry, but a good pie, especially with a crumble topping—none of that double-crust nonsense, it has to be a crumble with brown sugar and plenty of butter—well, he just loved it. \n\nI always used Granny Smith apples. The red ones turn to complete mush when you bake them. I learned that the hard way when I first got married. I made a pie for my mother-in-law using Red Delicious, and it came out looking like applesauce in a pastry shell. She didn't say a word, of course, she just took one bite, gave me that tight little smile she always did, and pushed her plate away. She was a very particular woman. Her house was always spotless, you could eat off the floors. She used to iron her sheets. Can you imagine? Who has the time to iron bedsheets? \n\nI certainly don't. Although I suppose I have plenty of time now, since I'm not working anymore. I retired from the bakery... well, it wasn't the bakery I mentioned before, it was the front office at the dental clinic. I worked there for twenty-two years. Dr. Miller was a saint, but his partner, Dr. Evans... oh, he was a piece of work. Always snapping at the hygienists... \n\nWait, what were the words again? Apple, table... and... oh, I've lost the last one. Penny! That was it. Apple, table, penny. I'll try to hold onto them.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me how you go about managing your monthly household bills and finances?\n\nPatient: Well, I just sit down at the kitchen table on the first of the month with my checkbook. I still write paper checks for the water and the electric. My daughter keeps telling me to do it all online, but I just don't trust putting my bank numbers on the internet. You hear so many stories about hackers these days. Just last week on the evening news, they were talking about people having their whole identities stolen by teenagers sitting in basements halfway across the world. It's terrifying, really. \n\nThat's why I like my paper checks. Though I will say, the mail delivery has been terribly inconsistent lately. Our regular mailman, Gary, retired in April. He was wonderful, always made sure the envelopes weren't bent or left out in the rain. He told me he was moving to Florida to live near his sister. Florida is much too humid for me, though. My hair just frizzes right up the second I step off the plane in Orlando. We went to Disney World back in 1988, and it rained every single afternoon at exactly three o'clock... \n\nBut anyway, my day-to-day life is pretty steady. Outside of dealing with the remodel, I still keep up with my church gatherings, book club, household routines, and family plans the way I usually do. We're reading a historical fiction novel for book club this month. It's about World War II, which is fine, but Mary—she's the one who picked it—always chooses these terribly depressing stories. I prefer a nice mystery, like Agatha Christie. They just don't write mysteries like they used to, do they? It's all so violent and graphic on television now... \n\nOh dear, I'm sorry, doctor. I'm rambling about Mary's books. Did you ask me about my bills? Yes, I just write the checks and put them in the mailbox.\n\nDoctor: Could you please repeat those three words I asked you to remember a moment ago?\n\nPatient: Three words... oh, yes, you did ask me to remember those, didn't you? Let me just think for a second. I know I repeated them back to you... it's funny how things just slip right out of your mind these days. \n\nMy daughter keeps telling me I need to do those Sudoku puzzles to keep my brain sharp. The ones in the local paper are just about my speed, though they recently changed the editor and the clues have gotten so terribly strange. Last Tuesday, there was a clue about some popular rap music singer, and how on earth am I supposed to know that? I mostly just listen to the oldies station. They play Frank Sinatra on Sunday mornings. My husband and I used to dance in the kitchen to Frank Sinatra while the Sunday roast was in the oven. He was such a wonderful dancer... he had two left feet when we first met, but he actually took secret lessons just for our wedding. The look on my mother's face when he spun me around the dance floor... \n\nOh, goodness. I'm sorry, doctor. The words. I'm afraid they've completely vanished. I don't remember a single one of them.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to manage all of your own household chores, such as cooking full meals, cleaning the house, and doing your own laundry without any help?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do all of that myself. I cook, I clean, I do the laundry. I've always been very particular about my laundry, especially the whites. You have to use just a splash of bleach, but not too much or it ruins the fibers. My neighbor, Susan, she uses those little plastic pod things you just throw in the machine. I don't trust them. How do you know what's really in them? Plus, I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office—not Dr. Miller's office, my new dentist, Dr. Patel, he's very gentle—anyway, the article said those pods are terrible for the environment. \n\nSpeaking of the environment, have you noticed how strange the weather has been lately? It was so warm last week, I actually went out and pruned my rosebushes. I have a beautiful climbing rose on the trellis by the back door. It's called a 'New Dawn' rose, a lovely pale pink. My grandmother had them in her garden when I was a little girl. She lived in this big Victorian house with a wraparound porch, and we would sit out there in the evenings drinking lemonade. Real lemonade, not that powdered stuff. \n\nOh, but you were asking about chores. Yes, I manage just fine. I still make a full dinner every night, even if it's just for me. Last night I made a nice meatloaf.\n\nDoctor: I am a little confused—earlier you mentioned your husband showing you receipts, but you also mentioned he has passed away; could you clarify if your husband is currently living with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you're asking me, doctor, or why that's confusing to you. We were just talking about the receipts. \n\nBut goodness, speaking of receipts, have you noticed how ridiculously long they make them at the pharmacy these days? And it got completely soaked because the delivery boy didn't even bother to put it in a plastic bag, he just threw it right in a puddle. Kids today just don't take any pride in their work. When I got my first job at the bakery downtown right out of high school, I made sure every single pastry box was tied perfectly with baker's twine... \n\nOh, excuse me. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about the bakery?\n\nDoctor: Earlier, I mentioned a few things to you, but I'd like to try something different: could you please remember these three words for me—Apple, Table, and Penny—and I will ask you to repeat them back to me in a few minutes?\n\nPatient: Apple, table, penny. Yes, I can remember those. Apple... you know, I used to make the most wonderful apple pies from scratch. My husband, God rest his soul, he would always ask for one on his birthday instead of a cake. He said cakes were too dry, but a good pie, especially with a crumble topping—none of that double-crust nonsense, it has to be a crumble with brown sugar and plenty of butter—well, he just loved it. \n\nI always used Granny Smith apples. The red ones turn to complete mush when you bake them. I learned that the hard way when I first got married. I made a pie for my mother-in-law using Red Delicious, and it came out looking like applesauce in a pastry shell. She didn't say a word, of course, she just took one bite, gave me that tight little smile she always did, and pushed her plate away. She was a very particular woman. Her house was always spotless, you could eat off the floors. She used to iron her sheets. Can you imagine? Who has the time to iron bedsheets? \n\nI certainly don't. Although I suppose I have plenty of time now, since I'm not working anymore. I retired from the bakery... well, it wasn't the bakery I mentioned before, it was the front office at the dental clinic. I worked there for twenty-two years. Dr. Miller was a saint, but his partner, Dr. Evans... oh, he was a piece of work. Always snapping at the hygienists... \n\nWait, what were the words again? Apple, table... and... oh, I've lost the last one. Penny! That was it. Apple, table, penny. I'll try to hold onto them.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me how you go about managing your monthly household bills and finances?\n\nPatient: Well, I just sit down at the kitchen table on the first of the month with my checkbook. I still write paper checks for the water and the electric. My daughter keeps telling me to do it all online, but I just don't trust putting my bank numbers on the internet. You hear so many stories about hackers these days. Just last week on the evening news, they were talking about people having their whole identities stolen by teenagers sitting in basements halfway across the world. It's terrifying, really. \n\nThat's why I like my paper checks. Though I will say, the mail delivery has been terribly inconsistent lately. Our regular mailman, Gary, retired in April. He was wonderful, always made sure the envelopes weren't bent or left out in the rain. He told me he was moving to Florida to live near his sister. Florida is much too humid for me, though. My hair just frizzes right up the second I step off the plane in Orlando. We went to Disney World back in 1988, and it rained every single afternoon at exactly three o'clock... \n\nBut anyway, my day-to-day life is pretty steady. Outside of dealing with the remodel, I still keep up with my church gatherings, book club, household routines, and family plans the way I usually do. We're reading a historical fiction novel for book club this month. It's about World War II, which is fine, but Mary—she's the one who picked it—always chooses these terribly depressing stories. I prefer a nice mystery, like Agatha Christie. They just don't write mysteries like they used to, do they? It's all so violent and graphic on television now... \n\nOh dear, I'm sorry, doctor. I'm rambling about Mary's books. Did you ask me about my bills? Yes, I just write the checks and put them in the mailbox.\n\nDoctor: Could you please repeat those three words I asked you to remember a moment ago?\n\nPatient: Three words... oh, yes, you did ask me to remember those, didn't you? Let me just think for a second. I know I repeated them back to you... it's funny how things just slip right out of your mind these days. \n\nMy daughter keeps telling me I need to do those Sudoku puzzles to keep my brain sharp. She bought me a whole thick book of them for Christmas, but I just can't make heads or tails of all those numbers. I've always preferred crosswords. The ones in the local paper are just about my speed, though they recently changed the editor and the clues have gotten so terribly strange. Last Tuesday, there was a clue about some popular rap music singer, and how on earth am I supposed to know that? I mostly just listen to the oldies station. They play Frank Sinatra on Sunday mornings. My husband and I used to dance in the kitchen to Frank Sinatra while the Sunday roast was in the oven. He was such a wonderful dancer... he had two left feet when we first met, but he actually took secret lessons just for our wedding. The look on my mother's face when he spun me around the dance floor... \n\nOh, goodness. I'm sorry, doctor. The words. I'm afraid they've completely vanished. I don't remember a single one of them.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to manage all of your own household chores, such as cooking full meals, cleaning the house, and doing your own laundry without any help?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do all of that myself. I cook, I clean, I do the laundry. I've always been very particular about my laundry, especially the whites. You have to use just a splash of bleach, but not too much or it ruins the fibers. My neighbor, Susan, she uses those little plastic pod things you just throw in the machine. I don't trust them. How do you know what's really in them? Plus, I read an article in a magazine at the dentist's office—not Dr. Miller's office, my new dentist, Dr. Patel, he's very gentle—anyway, the article said those pods are terrible for the environment. \n\nSpeaking of the environment, have you noticed how strange the weather has been lately? It was so warm last week, I actually went out and pruned my rosebushes. I have a beautiful climbing rose on the trellis by the back door. It's called a 'New Dawn' rose, a lovely pale pink. My grandmother had them in her garden when I was a little girl. She lived in this big Victorian house with a wraparound porch, and we would sit out there in the evenings drinking lemonade. Real lemonade, not that powdered stuff. \n\nOh, but you were asking about chores. Yes, I manage just fine. I still make a full dinner every night, even if it's just for me. Last night I made a nice meatloaf.\n\nDoctor: I am a little confused—earlier you mentioned your husband showing you receipts, but you also mentioned he has passed away; could you clarify if your husband is currently living with you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you're asking me, doctor, or why that's confusing to you. We were just talking about the receipts. \n\nBut goodness, speaking of receipts, have you noticed how ridiculously long they make them at the pharmacy these days? I went in on Tuesday just to buy some dental floss—the waxed kind, mind you, because the unwaxed always frays and gets stuck between my back molars. Anyway, I bought this one little plastic container of floss, and the young girl at the register handed me a piece of paper that must have been three feet long! All those coupons printed on the back that nobody ever looks at. It's just a terrible waste of trees. \n\nMy husband always hated waste. He grew up during the tail end of the Depression, you see, so they saved absolutely everything in his house. Tin foil, rubber bands, those little square plastic tabs that come on the bread loaves... I actually still have a whole drawer in the kitchen just full of bread tabs. Sometimes I use them to scrape baked-on cheese off the casserole dishes. They work like a charm without scratching the glass. I make a wonderful tuna noodle casserole, by the way. I use the crushed-up potato chips on top to give it a nice salty crust. My daughter says nobody eats casseroles anymore and that they're out of style, but I think that's just silly. It's comfort food, plain and simple. Do you like casseroles, doctor?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I really don't understand why you keep asking me the exact same thing, doctor. I just told you, he shows me the receipts. It is all right there on the paper. 

People always need everything on paper these days anyway. Like at the bank. I went down to the branch on Friday to deposit a check my sister sent me for my birthday. She always sends a check, which is nice of her, but she puts it in one of those cheap greeting cards from the dollar store. The glitter just falls off everywhere. I opened it on the kitchen island and I am still wiping up little pink specks. It gets stuck to the sponge, and then you just end up spreading glitter all over the other counters when you try to clean up. 

Anyway, the bank teller, this young man with a terrible haircut, he actually asked to see my driver's license. I have been banking at that exact branch for twenty-five years. I asked him if he didn't recognize me, but he said the computer makes them scan it. Everything is computers and scanning now. You can't even call the pharmacy without talking to a robot for ten minutes. I tried to call the other day and the machine just kept saying it didn't understand my voice. I had to press zero about five times before a real human being picked up the phone.
